\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\phi\left(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[7.7778pt]{\hbox to6.75pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to6.75pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=6.75pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[7.7778pt]{\hbox to6.75pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to6.75pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=6.75pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[6.25002pt]{\hbox to5.20834pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to5.20834pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=5.20834pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[5.41673pt]{\hbox to4.09743pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to4.09743pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=4.09743pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}}\phi+m^{2}\phi\right)
. In the case of (
96
) we do not take just the linear portion
\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\varphi\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[7.7778pt]{\hbox to6.75pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to6.75pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=6.75pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[7.7778pt]{\hbox to6.75pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to6.75pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=6.75pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[6.25002pt]{\hbox to5.20834pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to5.20834pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=5.20834pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[5.41673pt]{\hbox to4.09743pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to4.09743pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=4.09743pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}}\varphi
, but rather use the full free gravity theory
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\mathcal{L}_{G}
, complete with its higher kinetic vertices and negative mass dimensions.
Of course these features will technically be fatal to the QFT at high energies, but they are already infamous in quantum gravity, and an attempt to address them here falls well outside our scope.
In both theories the vertices mediated by
b_{n}
and the matter
J
form the interaction
H_{I}
. Denoting the ground state of the free theories by
|0\rangle
, the scattering of the vacuum into the vacuum
\langle 0|S|0\rangle
can be given by the path integral representation

\langle 0|U_{I}(-\infty,\infty)|0\rangle=\frac{Z[J]}{Z_{0}},

(98)

where the generating functional becomes, in the case of (
96
),

\displaystyle Z[J]

\displaystyle\equiv\int\mathcal{D}\varphi\exp\left[i\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\mathcal{L}_{{T}}\right]

(99)

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\varphi\exp\left[-\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{{E}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{{G}}+\mathcal{L}_{{M}}\right)\right],

and we can (if needed) Wick rotate into the Euclidean action. The normalisation is given by
Z_{0}\equiv Z[0]
.
Note that the time evolution operator is the time-ordered exponent

U_{I}(-\infty,\infty)\equiv\mathcal{T}\left\{\exp\left[-i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}tH_{I}(t)\right]\right\}.

(100)

The field
\varphi
as it appears on the RHS of (
99
) is not an operator, but
H_{I}
in (
100
) will be constructed from the position-space representation
\varphi_{I}(x)
, of the field operator in the Heisenberg picture of the theory
\mathcal{L}_{G}

H_{I}(t)\equiv-\int\mathrm{d}^{3}xJ(x)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}b_{n}{\varphi_{I}(x)}^{n}.

(101)

In the perturbative QFT, we would like to expand both within this interaction Hamiltonian, and in powers thereof stemming from the exponent, the relevant ‘small’ quantity being powers of the reciprocal Planck mass in the couplings
b_{n}
.
We give examples in
Fig.

18
of some diagrams which might then arise if ultraviolet considerations can somehow be overlooked.

How can this setup be related to energy? We will consider that a
static
source
J({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}})
switches on and off adiabatically within the function
J(x)
, and is essentially present for the comparatively long interval
T
, i.e.

J(x)\equiv j(t)J({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}),\quad-T/2<t<T/2\implies j(t)=1.

(102)

For the case of (
95
), it is shown in
Coleman 2011
that we can expect the transient imposition of the source in this way to ‘break’ the QFT, in that the vacuum-vacuum scattering acquires a divergent phase as we send
{T\to\infty}

\langle 0|U_{I}(-\infty,\infty)|0\rangle=e^{-i(\gamma_{-}+\gamma_{+}+E_{J}T)},

(103)

where
\gamma_{+}
and
\gamma_{-}
are finite shifts restulting from the adiabatic process. The QFT can be fixed by adding a constant counterterm to
H_{I}
, but one can keep it broken so that the constant
E_{J}
is recovered through the angular velocity of the path integral in (
98
), and equated with the
potential
of
J({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}})
.

Considering still the case of (
95
), how can this path integral in (
98
) be efficiently evaluated? The elegant solution is presented in
Peskin and Schroeder 2005
. One can ultralocally shift the field
\phi
by the solution to its own (sourced) field equation, effectively completing the square in the exponent within
Z[J]
. The
\mathcal{D}\phi
integration then cancels over
Z_{0}
, and the energy
E_{J}
from the logarithm is the autocorrelation of the source with the Klein–Gordon propagator as a kernel. It is very natural to consider a
J({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}})
distribution comprising a pair of point sources, as set out for the case of mass-energy sources in (
27
). In that case regularised self-energies may be discarded from the autocorrelation: by retaining only the cross-terms one is left (once the time is integrated over) with the Yukawa (or Coulomb) potential at the source separation distance. By general considerations, this static portion of the Klein–Gordon propagator may also be obtained via the path integral representation of the static two-point function. We assume some version of this line of thinking to have motivated the lattice techniques employed in
GEFC/
Deur 2017
.

But is it safe to assume the above analysis holds for the (generalised) GEFC scalar in (
96
), as it does for (
95
)? To find out, let us attempt to ‘separate’ the path integral defined by
Eqs.

99
,
100
and
101
along lines similar to those used in
Peskin and Schroeder 2005
. We ask if this is possible, under an ultralocal redefinition

\varphi\to\varphi+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}c_{nm}\mathcal{J}^{n-m}\varphi^{m}.

(104)

In (
104
) we introduce the
nonlocal
source

\mathcal{J}(x)\equiv i\int\mathrm{d}^{4}y\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.14795pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.14795pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83153pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7368pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.14795pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.14795pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83153pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7368pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.14795pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.14795pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83153pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7368pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.14795pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.14795pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83153pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7368pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{{F}}}}}(x-y)J(y),

(105)

where
\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}(x-y)
is the (massless) Feynman propagator

\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}(x-y)\equiv\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}p\frac{e^{-i\mathchoice{p^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{p^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{p^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{p^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(\mathchoice{x^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{x^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{x^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{x^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}-\mathchoice{y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\script (106)

which is a Green’s function of the d’Alembertian with normalisation
{\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[7.7778pt]{\hbox to6.75pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to6.75pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=6.75pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[7.7778pt]{\hbox to6.75pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to6.75pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=6.75pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[6.25002pt]{\hbox to5.20834pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to5.20834pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=5.20834pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\makebox[5.41673pt]{\hbox to4.09743pt{\vrule width=6.77002pt\kern-6.77002pt\vbox to4.09743pt{\hrule height=6.77002pt,width=4.09743pt\vss\hrule height=13.54004pt}\kern-16.92505pt\vrule width=16.92505pt}}}}\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\text{F}}}}}(x-y)=-i\delta^{4}(x-y)}
.
Note that (
104
) is
induced
by the presence of the source, and connects smoothly to the identity as the source is switched off.

After some lengthy calculations (see supplemental material in
Barker et al. 2022
), it turns out that that this is indeed possible for general
\mathcal{L}_{G}
, but
only
if we impose some unique restrictions on the matter coupling in
\mathcal{L}_{M}
, which read at the lowest perturbative orders

\displaystyle b_{2}

\displaystyle=-a_{2},

(107a)

\displaystyle b_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}(2a_{2}^{2}+3a_{3}),

(107b)

\displaystyle b_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(2a_{2}^{3}+3a_{2}a_{3}-2a_{4}).

(107c)

This seems at first glance to be quite promising: notwithstanding the concerns raised in
Section

II
, if some gauge choice, spacetime symmetry or perturbation scheme can be found in which
Eqs.

107a
to
107c
hold (and in which the relativistic model (
96
) is actually faithful to GR), then a transformation of the form (
104
) exists which would allow us to write

Z[J]=Z_{0}\exp\left[i\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{{M}}\right].

(108)

There would then be some natural functional counterpart
E[J]
of Coleman’s zero-point energy
E_{J}
introduced in (
103
)

\displaystyle E[J]

\displaystyle\equiv\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{i}{T}\ln\left(\frac{Z[J]}{J_{0}}\right)

(109)

\displaystyle=-\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{M}.

The formula (
109
) for
E[J]
, given in terms of (
110
), is determined purely by the matter source
J({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}})
, making
no reference
to the gravitational field
\varphi
. This would not only constitute a concrete theoretical basis on which to examine the validity of the lattice calculations in
GEFC/
Deur 2017
, it would also seem to suggest a new physically motivated programme for localising gravitational energy
13
13

13

We recall that covariant gravitational energy localisation is impossible
Barker et al. 2019
.
.

These hopes are quickly dashed. The separated Lagrangian can eventually be recovered after a long calculation

\displaystyle+\cdots.

(110)

The first term in (
110
) will tell us that the energy of two static point masses is given by the Coulomb potential, and encodes the linear, Newtonian part of (
96
). All the interesting corrections are pure surface terms, and it is easy to verify in hindsight that the conditions
Eqs.

107a
to
107c
are acting to ensure that the theory (
96
) is merely a polynomial reparameterisation of (
95
) in the massless limit. No other solutions are to be found.

It may be interesting to consider more serious attempts at gravitational energy localisation along the lines set out in this appendix, in which the gauge-invariant, tensorial (and low-energy-effective-field-theoretic) nature of GR is properly accounted for. We leave this somewhat daunting task to future work, and concede that, for the time being, the physical meaning of the lattice calculations in
GEFC/
Deur 2017
has not been fully resolved.

Appendix B
Tracking the GEFC vs PPN corrections

The expansion of
\mathcal{L}_{G}
as it is defined in (
3
) is more challenging than that of
\mathcal{L}_{M}
, since the Riemann curvature introduces two derivatives whose action on the PPN potentials must be interpreted in powers of the virial velocity. In order to extract, by means of these gradients, the factor of
\rho^{*}
in common with (
29
), the quantity
\sqrt{-g}R
is expanded to
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)
, producing in a semi-covariant notation

(111)

Once again, we keep track of the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
correction to
\mathchoice{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{00}}}}{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{00}}}}{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{00}}}}{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{00}}}}
via braces.
The expansion (
111
) can be simplified by a careful addition of surface terms, with the help of the identities

\displaystyle\dot{U}\equiv-\mbox{\boldmath$\nabla$}\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\nabla$}^{2}X\equiv-2U,

(112a)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\nabla$}^{2}U\equiv-\frac{\kappa}{2}\rho^{*},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\nabla$}^{2}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\equiv-\frac{\kappa}{2}\rho^{*}{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}},

(112b)

which can be obtained from (
26
). Here, (
112a
) encode the conserved matter current and superpotential while (
112b
) recover the matter and momentum sources.
Suitable use of
Eqs.

112a
and
112b
then yields the far simpler form (
30
), and to accelerate this manipulation (see supplemental material in
Barker et al. 2022
) we make use of the
xAct
,
xPert
and
xTras
tensor manipulation, perturbation and field theory software
Brizuela et al. 2009
;
Nutma 2014
. Note that
xAct
already has an advanced PPN implementation in
xPPN

Hohmann 2021
, but we happen not to use these tools in the current analysis.

Appendix C
Potential of a uniform density sphere

Following on from our discussion in
Section

III.1
, we now calculate the potential of a uniform density sphere. The purpose of this calculation will be to confirm — for a case where the exact solution to the EFEs in (
5
) is known — that the approximaion in (
36
) is actually
corrective
, i.e. reducing the error in the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
result.
By ‘potential’ here, we mean that we are going to calculate the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
correction to the
a_{1}
metric coefficient for a uniform sphere. Since in the axisymmetric work we have already adopted an ansatz at
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
which has a spatial metric which is conformally equivalent to flat space (although the conformal coefficient is generally a general function of both
R
and
z
), this means that the metric it is most convenient to work with here in the spherical case should be the one appropriate to
isotropic
space. We can write this in the exact case as

\displaystyle\mathchoice{g^{{{00}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{00}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{00}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{00}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\left(1+a_{1}\right)^{2},\quad\mathchoice{g^{{{11}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{11}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{11}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{11}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\left(1+b_{1}\right)^{2},

(113)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{g^{{{22}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{22}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{22}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{22}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\left(1+b_{1}\right)^{2},\quad\mathchoice{g^{{{33}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{33}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{33}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{33}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\left(1+b_{1}\right)^{2},

where
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
are functions of the spherical coordinate
r
.

So the problem we are dealing with in exact terms is finding the Schwarzschild interior and exterior solutions written in isotropic spatial coordinates. This is discussed in e.g. Section III.E of
Barker et al. 2019
and was first solved by Wyman in 1946
Wyman 1946
. Since the solutions are not well known, we give the results, transferred to our metric coefficients, as

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2{M_{G}}\left(3a^{3}-r^{2}a+{M_{G}}r^{2}\right)}{4a^{4}-4{M_{G}}a^{3}+4{M_{G}}r^{2}a-{M_{G}}^{2}r^{2}},

(114a)

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{{M_{G}}\left(12a^{2}+6a{M_{G}}+{M_{G}}^{2}-4r^{2}\right)}{(2a+{M_{G}})^{3}}.

(114b)

Here
{M_{G}}
is the ‘gravitational mass’ of the object, which has radius
a
, and these are related to the (constant) density
\rho
via

\rho=\rho_{0}=\frac{48{M_{G}}a^{3}}{\pi\left(2a+{M_{G}}\right)^{6}}.

(115)

(See
Barker et al. 2019
for a discussion of the different types of mass definition possible in this context.) The other quantity of interest is the pressure,
P
, which is not given by Wyman, but has the expression

\displaystyle P=

(116)

\displaystyle\frac{96a^{4}{M_{G}}^{2}(a-r)(a+r)}{\pi(2a+{M_{G}})^{6}\left(4a^{4}-4{M_{G}}a^{3}+4{M_{G}}r^{2}a-{M_{G}}^{2}r^{2}\right)}.

We can see this vanishes at the edge of the object, as we would expect.

So these quantities define the exact solution, and we now wish to see if our expression (
36
) works correctly in terms of moving us towards the exact solution, if we start from a
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
GEM type solution. Note we will only be applying this to the
a_{1}
coefficient. In the spherical case, it is possible to do the same for the
b_{1}
function which controls the spatially isotropic part of the metric, but while we are able to have a similar setup to this at
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
in the axisymmetric case, this does not persist to
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
since one soon finds that if the pressure is non-zero, then
b_{1}
has to be different to
c_{1}
, and a spatially isotropic metric is not possible.

The order in which we carry out solution is that we first solve (
37
) for
a_{1}^{f}
given the constant
\rho
, and then use this to get the pressure
P
at
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
from (
40
). These are then substituted in (
36
), enabling us to get
a_{1}^{s}
. The only boundary conditions that are perhaps non-obvious are that the derivatives of
a_{1}^{f}
and
a_{1}^{s}
are taken to match individually either side of the boundary of the object, at
r=a
. This enables us to say that the boundary conditions, in which the total
a_{1}
certainly has to have matching derivatives either side of
r=a
, to match the ‘singularity’ in
\rho
, which is in the form of a step, apply to each order in the expansion.

Carrying out this process, the solutions we find are

\displaystyle a_{1}^{f}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\frac{2\pi\rho_{0}\left(-r^{2}+3a^{2}\right)}{3}}&r<a,\\
{\frac{4\pi\rho_{0}a^{3}}{3r}}&r>a,\end{cases}

(117a)

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\frac{2\pi\rho_{0}^{2}(a-r)(a+r)}{3}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)}&r<a,\\
{0+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)}&r>a,\end{cases}

(117b)

\displaystyle a_{1}^{s}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\frac{8\pi^{2}\rho_{0}^{2}\left(r^{4}-6a^{2}r^{2}+12a^{4}\right)}{9}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)}&r<a,\\
{\frac{8\pi^{2}\rho_{0}^{2}a^{5}(a+6r)}{9r^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)}&r>a.\end{cases}

(117c)

The results for
a_{1}^{f}
and
P
are the expected Newtonian values, and we now want to see if the addition of the
a_{1}^{s}
corrections to the
a_{1}^{f}
is successful in bringing them closer to the exact results. We can do this in two ways. First by plotting the results and making a visual comparison, and secondly, and more accurately, by expanding to
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
and checking that this successfully brings the
a_{1}^{f}+a_{1}^{s}
and exact
a_{1}
results into alignment.

In

Fig.

19

Figure 19:
Comparison of the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
,
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
and exact results for the metric coefficient
a_{1}
(which is minus the potential at
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
), for a spherically symmetric case with
m=1/50
, and where the boundary of the object is at
r=a=1
. Black shows
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
, red shows the combination of
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
and
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
, and blue is exact.

we show a comparison of the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
,
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
and exact results for a case with
{M_{G}}=1/50
and
a=1
. (Note we do not need to specify
\rho_{0}
as well, since this is given by (
115
).) It can be seen that the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
results are definitely different from the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
result, but virtually indistinguishable from the exact result over nearly the entire range plotted, so the method appears to be working.

To carry out a series comparison, we need to decide on the appropriate variable to expand in. Since
r
can in principle be small, the variable we should work with is
{M_{G}}/a
, which we will denote
x
. Similarly we will denote
r/a
by
y
, since this simplifies the expressions, but we are not expanding in this. We find the following:

\displaystyle a_{1}^{f}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2}+\frac{3}{2}\right)x+\left(\frac{3y^{2}}{2}-\frac{9}{2}\right)x^{2}}+\ldots&r<a,\\
{\frac{x}{y}-\frac{3x^{2}}{y}}+\ldots&r>a,\end{cases}

(118a)

\displaystyle a_{1}^{f}+a_{1}^{s}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2}+\frac{3}{2}\right)x+\Big(-\frac{3}{2}y^{2}+\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}y^{4}\Big)x^{2}}+\ldots&r<a,\\
{\frac{x}{y}+\frac{x^{2}}{2y^{2}}}+\ldots&r>a,\end{cases}

(118b)

\displaystyle a_{1}^{\rm exact}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2}+\frac{3}{2}\right)x+\left(-\frac{3}{2}y^{2}+\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}y^{4}\right)x^{2}}+\ldots&r<a,\\
{\frac{x}{y}+\frac{x^{2}}{2y^{2}}}+\ldots&r>a.\end{cases}

(118c)

We see that indeed the exact and
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
results agree at
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
, so the method has been successful. Of course, these particular results are only applicable to the spherically symmetric case, and this is the case in which
GEFC/
Deur 2021a
would say there is no effect of the kind desired, since there is no flattening.

Turning now from the expression
(
36
) and instead considering the
radiative GEM
proxy in (
60
), as a first example let us try something very similar to the above analysis by calculating the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
gravitational
potential of a sphere of uniform density
\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}
and radius
a
. For any
spherically symmetric system, the integral solution in the first equation
in (
26
) may be written in the simple form

\displaystyle\Phi(r)=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\bigg(

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\int_{0}^{r}\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}(r^{\prime})r^{\prime 2}\,\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}

(119)

\displaystyle+\int_{r}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}(r^{\prime})r^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}\bigg).

By analogy, one may write (
61
) for any
spherically-symmetric system as

\displaystyle\delta\Phi(r)=\frac{9\kappa}{4}\bigg(

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\int_{0}^{r}\Phi(r^{\prime})\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}(r^{\prime})r^{\prime 2}\,\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}

(120)

\displaystyle+\int_{r}^{\infty}\Phi(r^{\prime})\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}(r^{\prime})r^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}\bigg)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right).

Evaluating the expression (
119
) for a sphere of uniform
density
\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}
and radius
a
, with ‘mass’
M^{\dagger}\equiv\frac{4}{3}\pi\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}a^{3}
, and substituting into
(
120
) yields

\displaystyle\Phi(r)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}-\frac{\kappa}{12}\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}(3a^{2}-r^{2})&=&-GM^{\dagger}(3a^{2}-r^{2})/2a^{3}&\mbox{for $r\leq a$},\\[2.84526pt]
-\frac{\kappa}{6}\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}a^{3}/r&=&-GM^{\dagger}/r&\mbox{for $r>a$},\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\delta\Phi(r)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}3\left(\frac{\kappa\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}}{4}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{5}{4}a^{4}-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}r^{2}+\frac{1}{20}r^{4}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)&=&3\left(\frac{3GM^{\dagger}}{2a^{3}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{5}{4}a^{4}-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}r^{2}+\frac{1}{20}r^{4}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)&\mbox{for $r\leq a$},\\
-\frac{12}{5}\left(\frac{\kappa\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}}{4}\right)^{2}a^{5}/r+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)&=&-\frac{12}{5}\left(\frac{3GM^{\dagger}}{2a^{3}}\right)^{2}a^{5}/r+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)&\mbox{for $r>a$}.\end{array}\right.

In particular, for
r>a
, one may write the gravitational potential
up to
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
as

\Phi(r)+\delta\Phi(r)=-\frac{GM^{\dagger}}{r}\left(1+\frac{27}{5}\frac{GM^{\dagger}}{a}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right).

(122)

As discussed in
Section

III.4
, a consequence of the radiative average is that we do not expect the correction in (
122
) to be strictly faithful to the exact result which would follow from
Eqs.

118b
and
118c
, even if the difference between
\mathchoice{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}{\rho^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{0}}}}
and
\mathchoice{\rho_{{{0}}}}{\rho_{{{0}}}}{\rho_{{{0}}}}{\rho_{{{0}}}}
is factored in: it is, however, straightforward to calculate, and perfectly comparable in magnitude.

Appendix D
The ‘bipartite’ harmonic gauge

To understand why the nonlinear gravitoelectric correction obtained in
Section

III.4
differs from the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
PPN correction, we must understand how the gauge choice which is developed over
Sections

I.2
and
III.3
departs from the exact harmonic gauge

\mathchoice{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}\mathchoice{\mathfrak{g}^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathfrak{g}^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathfrak{g}^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathfrak{g}^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=0,\quad\mathchoice{\mathfrak{g}^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathfrak{g}^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathfrak{g}^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathfrak{g}^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\equiv\sqrt{-g}\mathchoice{g^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},

(123)

at the same PN order.
From the outset, it is clear from
Section

I.2
that all gauges under consideration take
\mathchoice{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu}}}}{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu}}}}{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu}}}}{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu}}}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
, and so the nonlinear harmonic gauge from (
123
) demands

(125)

We thus identify in (
125
) the
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
departure from the harmonic gauge.
